ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e202	Honourable co-representatives! Former representative of the Central Party and Vice President of Stortinget, Edvard Grimstad, died on April 3rd this year. Edvard Grimstad was born in the Council of Eastfold on 29 March 1933. In his home municipality, he was rapporteur and rapporteur and an important driver of social development at the place of residence. This commitment he maintained throughout his life, even long after he became a rich politician and an important voice here on the Storting. Edvard Grimstad's long line of trust in Norwegian politics and organisational life has been important in many ways. He was elected to the Stortinget in 1989 and represented Eastfold for eight years, until 1997. At Stortinget he held a number of positions in various committees, including: the Defence Committee and the Justice Committee. From 1993 to 1997, he was vice president of the Stortinget. Edvard Grimstad was one of the bautaene in Norwegian politics. He belonged to the range of trustees who have contributed knowledge and wisdom to society through experience and practical approach to the great lines of Norwegian policy, a kind of quality many today demand. In the Center Party, his descendants say that Edvard Grimstad, by virtue of his commitment and his wisdom, has left behind traces that have shaped Norway in the best possible sense. It's a great certificate to bring. Even though the biggest issues he dealt with happened at Stortinget, those who knew him well, believe that it is wrong to point this out as the most important thing for Edvard Grimstad. Trustees in the Center Party say that the home state of Eastfold and Edvard Grimstad was one and the same in many respects. He traveled a lot around municipalities and towns in his home country. An acquaintance of Grimstad from Rakkestad told of the many times he came to the village, either as a state politician, a big man, or as a countyman. He did not come to throw shine or sank voices. He came to listen. And what he heard, he took with him. He was your ombudsman, whether you were big or small. The fact that he was in the home state of Eastfold when he left as a staging representative did not surprise anyone. Edvard Grimstad concluded his strong commitment to the close things where it all started. He was a countyman for five years, and the home state has a lot to thank him for. Edvard Grimstad was a politician with a great heart. When, as a young man in the 1960s, he traveled to Kenya with his wife, Lene, he was given further vision. The experiences and lessons imprinted on him for the rest of his life, and the love of Africa gave him an extra dimension. Here in Stortinget we have a lot to thank Edvard Grimstad for, and we would like to thank his entire family – first of all for sharing a dear husband and father with the nation's national assembly. We bring peace to Edvard Grimstad's memory. The representatives heard the President's commemorative speech.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e325	The Committee has examined a proposal from workers’ party representatives to make the use of children’s homes compulsory and to strengthen children’s homes. First, I would like to thank the Committee for its good cooperation in the work on this important matter. It is gratifying to experience that there is an overall committee dedicated to strengthening children's homes and contributing to making the use of children's homes compulsory. Where the majority of the Committee separates the Workers' Party from the Centre Party, the age limit for making the use of children's housing compulsory. The majority will make use of children's homes compulsory for children under 16, while the Workers' Party and the Centre will make use of children's homes compulsory for children under 18, and I expect them to come back to that. There has also been a strong historical consensus about the political effort to strengthen the legal certainty of children and young people who have been subjected to violence and abuse or have witnessed such. This joint commitment resulted in the creation of the first children’s home in 2007, and today there are ten children’s homes around the country. The main objective of the establishment of children’s housing is to strengthen the legal certainty of the child. There is also a clear and sincere commitment by the government in this matter. It is made clear both in the letter of reply from the Minister of Justice to the Committee in connection with the examination of the proposal for a representative and in an inter-pelling debate on the subject in winter. Violence and sexual assault are serious crimes that can have serious and long-lasting consequences for vulnerable children. Therefore, it is important that these types of cases are taken seriously and treated as closely as possible from the point of view of origin, no matter where in the country where the child is located. National figures from children’s homes show that the average time of interrogation for sexual assault is 52 days, which is 38 days longer than the statutory deadline. It is not difficult to understand how stressful it is for children who have experienced such events. There is an overall committee which is committed to reducing the waiting time for judicial hearings. There are good examples in the process that show that if the police are betting on these kinds of issues, so are the results. Own judicial hearing coordinators are an example of this. Another important aspect is the presence of the Judge in the hearing. In order to ensure that the hearings can be conducted more efficiently and as quickly as necessary, the Committee wishes the responsibility for the hearings to be transferred to the police. In such a case, the presence of a judge would no longer be necessary for the conduct of the hearing. Children’s homes have established good procedures and have become incredibly good at facilitating the hearings, and they have also established good procedures to care for the children at risk of violence and to guide the assistance system around the children. There is an overall committee that supports the job done by children's homes. There is therefore a clear expectation from Stortinget that priority should be given to the state children’s home and that the conditions should be facilitated by all parties in order to reduce the waiting time from notification to interrogation in the course of this process. But in any case, the most important thing to succeed in bringing down the waiting time for a court hearing is that the police prioritise the investigation of violence and abuse against children, and that the police prioritise the use of their trained referees for this type of case. Then why does the majority set the limit at 16 years? Indeed, for the Right, the Advance Party and the Christian People's Party, it has been important to ensure that for the most vulnerable children, judicial hearings are conducted within the deadline. We also strongly support the report presented to the government in 2012 following the evaluation of the State Children's House. The children's houses have a great deal of stuff. The children’s home in Oslo had an increase of 62 p.m. in the first three months of 2014. compared to the same period in 2013. This is a tendency that is equal throughout the country. This suggests that it is necessary to strengthen the capacity of the State Children’s House. I also feel that it is right to say, although not so much has been mentioned in the written comments, that children’s homes apply not only to children at risk of violence but also to those with mental retardation – what has been commented on here about children, of course, also to those with mental retardation. I am pleased that today, through this treatment, we have put in place a clear prioritisation of these kinds of issues. Our children shall we do all we can to protect them, and to ensure that those who experience being subjected to, or witness, violence or sexual assault are taken seriously and followed up as necessary.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e738	The judicial remedy system is the Stortinget's own compensation scheme. Through this, there will be upliftment to many people who in childhood were subjected to injustice on the part of society, the human being that the state was responsible for caring for. Instead, they were subjected to selection and manipulation. No money — no matter how much money — can give back these human childhoods, but through this system, society gives humans some upliftment for the injustice they have suffered. In this there is also a symbolic action, a value-based action, simply to show that society sees them, that the Greatness sees them, that there is also an uproar in this. Today we are discussing the annual report for 2013. The number of applications received under this scheme in 2013 shows that the number of cases still falls slightly. Last year there were two selections in work, but they indicate that from 2014 there will be only one selection in work, due to just the low entry into new things. The Committee would therefore stress the importance of people who are intended to apply for legal compensation – primarily for the purpose of this upliftment to the individual, but secondary because it more precisely shows the extent of those who have come out particularly unfortunate, and where there is some criticism on the part of the public. It is a joint committee which underlines the importance of the judicial remedy system and the fullness of justice.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e761	At the request of the Judicial Committee, the President will propose that the speaking time be limited to 5 minutes to each party group and 5 minutes to the government. Furthermore, the President will propose that five lines of reply be given after comments by members of the Government within the allocated speaking time, and that those on the list of speakers, in addition to the allocated speaking time, have a speaking time of up to 3 minutes. – It shall be deemed to have been adopted. The first speaker is the representative, Ulf Leirstein, who is given the floor on behalf of the President-in-Office, Jan Arild Ellingsen.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e770	There has been a long fight to get the law change that we are discussing today. For a long time, many have fought hard to remove the rules of old age for gross criminal acts. Many have experienced that the limitation period has led to the release of perpetrators from punishment, and many of us have failed to understand why such abominable, criminal acts were to expire. As I said, this has been a struggle many have fought for many years, and many who have been subjected to abuse, and many relatives have been at the forefront of the fight to eliminate the limitation period. I am pleased to note that today this battle is crowned with victory. Also, as a representative of the Progress Party, I cannot fail to mention that the Progress Party was entirely alone when we first proposed it in 2003, and we were alone in the fight to get this into the criminal law of 2005. But today, I will first and foremost be pleased that the government, the committee and the Stortinget have provided swift treatment of the law, and that this battle can now be crowned with victory. I would also like to take the opportunity to commend the Christian People's Party for putting the issue on the agenda quickly after the election through a representative proposal earlier this spring. Although the Government Platform is aware that these are changes that will be put in place during this period, it is very pleasant that all parties represented in the Judicial Committee now see the importance of this legislative change for the victims. The Judicial Committee has therefore taken responsibility as a college and has ensured that this matter is dealt with before the summer. There's a lot of reason why this change is urgent. Both the government and the Justice Committee have gone as far as the Constitution § 97 allows in the matter of giving the law retroactive effect. The result is that all serious violations and killings that are not yet out of date will not be obsolete after the law enters into force on 1 July. However, the Constitution sets the limit in such a way that matters that are already out of date under current legislation cannot be reinvigorated. The longer we wait with this bill, the more matters will become obsolete, and then the victims and the relatives will lose the opportunity to get the case resolved and the offender punished. There are a number of serious offences that will be covered by this change. Cases of killing, rape and sexual intercourse with children under 14 and 16 years of age are completely exempted from the rules on parentality following the change. In other words, these crimes will always be punishable, no matter how long has passed since the deed took place. The amendment will also ensure that the limitation period does not start until the victims reach the age of 18 in cases of violence in close relations, forced marriages and trafficking in human beings. In addition, the new Penal Code of 2005 has already adopted outlaws for trafficking in human beings. We have to take inwards as societies, that time doesn't always heal all wounds. Some crimes are so brutal, so fundamentally inhuman, that as a society we should not allow punishment to go away because of procedural rules. The victim of violence who has lost his security, the grieving and lonely widow without a reply, the mother of an abused child: these are people who have gone through the worst imaginable. We can't give these people back what they've lost, but we have a duty as a society to do what we can to set up. These victims and relatives deserve answers, they deserve respect, and they deserve not least to be taken seriously. I am therefore pleased that, through the decision here today, we are taking a step in the right direction to improve the situation of the victims in this country. It's really time for the victims to be taken seriously. Norway has been very good at building up criminal services to rehabilitate and protect the rights of criminals, but the rights of the victims have not received the attention they deserve. Of course, it is important to provide for rehabilitation in criminal matters, but ethically, it is even more important to care for the victims. I must point out that unlike the criminal, a victim is completely innocent in the situation that has arisen. Society has a fundamental responsibility for ensuring the safety of its citizens, and when the population is subjected to abuse, society should therefore take greater responsibility for the victims than it is today. The decision we are making here today is the first step on the road, but the Progress Party will continue to work to launch a series of measures to improve the situation of victims. This is such a clearly necessary legislative change that it is really almost shameful that we have waited until 2014 to get it in place. The size should have taken responsibility at a much earlier time. The Progress Party has a good conscience. Today more people are going to get it, but I think they should get it earlier. This room had a wide-ranging debate on these changes in the context of the 2005 Criminal Code, and the issue has also been addressed in a number of questions, proposals and proposals from the Progress Party. If the other parties wanted it, we could have prevented ten years of serious crime. However, I shall conclude by commending the Justice Committee for its good cooperation in this matter, so that we can bring about important and necessary legislative changes.

This increases the risk of a court decision becoming incorrect. In criminal matters, that consideration weighs heavily. And if one is aware of the uncertainty of the evidence, it will often lead to such a doubt that the prosecution ends in acquittal. ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e995	The Right Party, the Center Party and the Advance Party have now agreed to do something about the sadly increasing systematic organisation of the begging that we have seen in Norway. A large majority would like to welcome this, because many people experience begging as a real problem in their community, like as a source of insecurity, and in some cases a source of crime. The proposal gives rise to some strong reactions, which are both understandable and predictable. They are understandable because you can immediately believe that everyone who asks for a ticket to the bus should now be jailed. They're predictable because you've heard about the scare images so many times before. Now it's going to be criminal to be poor, now it's going to be forbidden to ask for help. Of course it's not like that. It's the systematic and almost professionalized beggar that we want to get under control. So it just has to be said that the beggar question is a very difficult question. That's a difficult question for many reasons. We in the Right have doubted the conclusion. Prohibiting certain forms of begging involves a difficult balance between different ideological views, justice policy issues and social considerations. On the one hand, the clear starting point is that people should initially be free to do whatever they want, as long as they don't bother their surroundings. When this starting point meets concrete information that many beg systematically, with targeted methods, and like to have branches of criminal networks, the question suddenly becomes much more difficult. It's also very difficult to accept a claim that this is so terriblely illiberal. No one shall prohibit poverty, no one shall prohibit the request for assistance in an emergency. But it has to be said that there is a significant difference in asking for help and doing begging almost as a job. It also has to be recognized by the most liberal of us. When beggaring is carried out systematically and continuously, it is obvious that at some point it will become too disruptive to the environment. That means that at some point we have to take account of the others in society as well. Very much of the activity we see in society today is subject to some kind of rule or regulation. Just to accept that our streets will become a arena of systematic begging, without also subjecting the kind of activity certain rules, is at the limit of naive. Nor is it that allowing begging in itself is so very solidarity or humane. The beggar ban does not solve the poverty problem at all, but it does not really do begging either. In a society like Norwegian, we give people help in completely different ways, by completely different means. It can be done to the same extent as before, whether you get a beg ban or not. Now that we have opted for the introduction of a ban on systematic begging, it is because, after a difficult overall assessment, it is for us to consider crime prevention and social order more than to consider that any Norwegian citizen or EEA citizen should be free to use our streets to engage in permanent begging.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e1035	This case began with the introduction of a bill from the government that deals with the possibility of allowing municipalities to adopt local prohibitions against begging through the police regulations. I'll come back to this. The central party programmed at the national meeting last year once again that we want to introduce the general ban on begging again. There's no one who's ever gotten out of social trouble by begging or by teaching their kids to beg. When Norway provides aid to poor countries, the aim is often to ensure better health provision and to implement the vaccine programme. The second main objective is to facilitate people to manage themselves in the short and long term, through, for example: supporting projects offering schooling for young people who would otherwise not have had a school offer. I think it's incredibly important that Norway target its efforts to prevent and reduce social and economic hardship. It is therefore clear from point C of the opinion of the majority of the committee – consisting of the Right, the Progress Party and the Center Party – that the government is asking the government to ensure targeted social measures against beggars in Norway. Furthermore, we ask that the project resources transferred by Norway through the EEA system be clarified and used to improve the economic and social situation of the poorest. This theme will be presented in the multiple comments in the Preferences dialog. A beg ban does not pose any problem for e.g. sports teams, school classes and voluntary organisations selling lotteries or collecting money for tours or non-profit purposes. ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e1476	The Committee has considered Prop. 57 L, Act of Conflict Council. It is a unanimous committee that supports the proposal in this proposition. The proposal proposes a new conflict resolution law and amendments to, inter alia: the criminal law, the criminal procedure law and the criminal enforcement law. In its work on the proposal, the Committee was made aware of an inconsistency between the comment in Prop. 135 L for 2010–2011 and the wording of the Act under the responsibility of the Ministry of Justice and Defence. Therefore, the Committee also promotes proposals for changes to the legislative text as shown in the vote. In this proposition, the Government proposes a new conflict resolution law that reflects current practice and facilitates the future development of the conflict resolution system. The proposition targets both civil and criminal cases. However, the main emphasis of the proposals is on the use of re-establishing procedures in criminal matters. The proposals in this proposition are part of the follow-up to Prop. 135 L for 2010–2011, children and punishment, which was unanimously adopted by the Stortinget in December 2011. The increase thus adopted a new type of punishment, juvenile punishment, as an alternative to unconditional imprisonment and more stringent community penalties for offenders between 15 and 18 years of age at the time of action. In order to ensure a coherent chain of individualised response options anchored in the conflict council, the government proposes a new criminal law response for young offenders, referred to as youth follow-up. The new response shall be used for offences which are not so serious as to give rise to the issue of juvenile punishment. The implementation shall take place in the Council of Conflict in accordance with the same pattern as the Youth Penalty, but in most cases with shorter implementation times and less interventional measures. A lawbreaker shall, inter alia: attend a major youth meeting. Through a meeting with offended people and other interested parties, the offender must participate actively and through this will become more aware of the consequences of his crimes. In addition, the young person must demonstrate the willingness to take responsibility for his/her actions by agreeing to an obliging youth plan. The plan shall be designed individually and shall include follow-up and control measures. The plan will also contain support measures across sectors and levels of administration. The starting point for action will be the crime committed and the life situation of the offender. This means that both juvenile and youth follow-up are based on early efforts by both the justice sector and various municipal and state aid agencies. The response is based on experience from local cooperation models that have been tested to follow young offenders in the most appropriate way possible. Reference is made in particular to youth contracts, drug agreements and various forms of follow-up team. In addition to youth follow-up, follow-up in conflict advice is proposed as a new criminal law response. The reaction is directed at offenders over 18 years of age and, like youth follow-up, involves an extension of the current mediation system to include follow-up over a specified period of time in appropriate cases. I also point to the fact that the case has been at the hearing, and the main impression of the hearing is that the hearing bodies are in favour of the modernisation of the conflict resolution law and of the legislative proposals on follow-up in the conflict council as a new criminal law response.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e1495	It's good again to discuss some things there's unanimity about! The Christian People's Party is very pleased with this matter and is pleased with the unanimity that exists. We are very positive about youth follow-up and for follow-up in conflict councils. We very much believe in recovery processes. It's good for the victim, it makes it easier to move on, and it's also good for the perpetrator, because he might then understand the seriousness of what he's done and find a new course more easily. So overall, we are very pleased with the direction that the Minister of Justice is pointing out. ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e1593	On 9 November, as I said, 25 years have passed since the fall of the Berlin Wall. The people of East Germany had had enough of the oppression and gathered under the slogan «We are the people» – «We are the people». The force was irresistible, and the communist illusion was inconceivable against the peaceful desire of the people for freedom. There is very strong symbolism in this, and the 25th anniversary is a mark of democracy's victory over dictatorship. This interpellation is about East Germany’s spying against Norway. In the period 1969 to 1989, more than 300 Stasi agents reported Norwegian conditions. A total of 1,920 reports were sent to Communist East Germany, an enemy state. 85 pst. the reports concerned political issues, defence and intelligence in Norway. This invisible invasion of the country continued until the fall of the wall in 1989. Researchers at the Stasi Archive in Berlin believe that over 30 of the agents were Norwegian citizens. No one has been convicted in Norway. Have this relevance today, maybe ask someone. Absolutely – and to a very large extent, because this is part of our recent history that is mostly unexplored. The cold war has shaped our country both politically and culturally. We have to systematize and document the lessons from this period. What prompted dozens of Northmen to spy for an authoritarian social system and a hostile state, while they themselves could benefit from the freedom of a democracy and a rule of law? Was it so that as long as one was convinced that radical socialism represented the objective good, it could be accepted by some victims? The oppression was done in the name of the right doctrine, must know. Didn't you see the emergence of organizations like Stasi as alarming? When East Germany collapsed in 1989, it is estimated that Stasi had 91,000 employees and 189 000 unofficial agents. Stasi is considered one of the most effective intelligence organisations in its field and the East German population as the most monitored population ever. The first election in 2009 was the first to be born after the fall of the wall. They thus do not have their own reference points to the Cold War. A recent survey of Swedish young people shows that 22 pst. considers communism to be a democratic form of society. In the same survey, 95 pst. That they know about Auschwitz, but 90 pst. I don't know about Gulag. This may indicate that there are huge gaps in the emerging generation of basic knowledge about our recent history. This is reinforced by a lack of media and scientific attention on the theme. With some honourable exceptions, very few Norwegian journalists and researchers have been involved in this area. Last week, the government presented its action plan against radicalisation and violent extremism, an offensive and important document. It's interesting to read that many of the psychological and social factors that drive people into a radicalization process are now the same as before, regardless of political, ideological or religious driving forces. There is therefore a certain connection between the spy of the Cold War for Communism and today’s foreign warriors. So the Swarmerie of the authoritarian is not new, but if we are to prevent the swarm from becoming an active act, we need to understand more about the motivation for radicalisation. So we have an important source of this understanding in our recent history. I think we have a deep obligation to search for the knowledge, truth and experience that has shaped our country, even if it may be uncomfortable or difficult to access, and especially when this can contribute to a better understanding of how to meet the driving forces that threaten our values and our society. Large parts of the Stasi archive are intact. You'd think it was easy to identify the Norwegian spies. It's not that simple. The documents that Stasi was going to destroy in the chaotic months after the fall of the wall were largely from foreign intelligence. In addition, the vast scale of the archive needs to be taken into account. Today, the archive represents 111 shelf-kilometres of documents, as well as millions of photographs, film recordings, audio recordings and other objects. In addition, more than 15 000 large sacks of mashed documents have been found. This interpellation is about the Norwegian authorities obtaining the necessary keys to be able to search intently in this huge archive. Such keys can be found in the so-called Rosenholz Cartotheque, which the CIA managed in uncertain circumstances during the breakup of East Germany in 1990. This is a collection of microfilmed card cards with information about interesting people in Stasis's eyes and about real Stasi spies. The card contains three types of cards: it's F-16 cards, which is a gross list of interesting people for Stasi, it's the so-called F-22 cards with active agents, and it's the so-called statistical cards, which contain code names, agent category, date of acquisition, motive for acquisition, etc. These cards constitute a whole and any handover to Norwegian authorities must include all three types. The CIA handed over the German section of the map to Germany in 2003. Let me clarify one important thing: the Rosenholz Cartoteket is primarily a personal register and, to a lesser extent, a spy register. The main feature of the cartoteque is that it allows targeted and much more precise searches to be made in the large Stasi archive. Let me also briefly get into criminal law. Spying expires after 25 years. The Rosenholz Cartotek was largely updated the last time in early 1988. It's more than 25 years ago, and it probably weakens the value of the map as a source of criminal prosecution in isolation. The case of the wall on 9 November 1989 is probably of little relevance to criminal law. On the other hand, 31 May 1990 is important. This day the password ‘Wittenberg’ was repeated three times over the Stasis radio connection — so silence. It meant the immediate closure of all foreign intelligence activities. Stasi was from that momentary story. Six days earlier, the same thing had happened with East Germany's military intelligence. It is against this background that I ask the Minister of Justice the following questions: Will the Council of State take the initiative to obtain from the U.S. authorities all registered information on Norwegian citizens in the Rosenholz Cartoteket?

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e1726	An overall committee joins Prop. 67 L for 2013–2014 on changes to the pollution law and the Criminal Procedures Act. These changes increase the level of penalties for illegal cross-border shipments of waste. New penalties are proposed for fines or prisons for up to two years, and the changes concern both illegal imports and exports of waste and attempts to do so. Illegal imports and exports of waste are a major environmental problem in many countries, and this is a serious crime that needs to be combated. The most common types of waste shipped are electronic waste, discarded vehicle, car parts and waste batteries — waste which may contain dangerous substances and which may harm nature and people if it is not properly treated. It may be possible to make money on waste, and therefore there has been a trend towards transport also out of Norway. The Committee is pleased that proposals for amendments made during consultations have also been included in the proposal. It is, as I said, an overall committee that agrees to this, and therefore I see no reason to do the debate any longer.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e1796	The question of a separate law on the ritual circumcision of boys has been a very difficult and demanding matter since it was sent to the hearing of the Red-Green Government in April 2011. The idea of ritual circumcision touches one of the innermost things in many of us. It gives us ethical and moral challenges about what is right and wrong when it comes to letting newborns and healthy boys be circumcised for religious reasons. The path to the broad consensus of the Health and Welfare Committee has been a long one. The same thing has the rounds in several of the batch groups. Once we have concluded together that it is right to offer cross-sections of boys in the specialist health service, it is primarily the concern of the children that has been crucial. Many have images on the Internet from TV reports that show the intersection of children on a kitchen table, without pain relief and performed by someone without the necessary health skills. Crossing boys takes place around the world — also in Norway. Today, we are not going to decide whether ritual circumcision is to be prohibited, but whether the service should be offered within the Public Health Service. The Party supports the proposal for a legal framework ensuring that such interception is carried out in a sound manner. We want to avoid cutting without the necessary pain relief and skilled health professionals present. It is reassuring that ritual circumcision carried out by persons other than those resulting from the law will be prohibited and can be punishable by fines or imprisonment for up to two years. New parents who want to circumcise their child need to be thoroughly informed about the risk of complications that are actually related to the intervention. They also need to be briefed on the prosecution that will take place if they choose to allow such interception to take place outside the specialist health service or approved private clinics. It is important to stress that intersection is strongly religiously connected, and that it is a common intervention in large parts of the world. It is especially Jews and Muslims who are circumcised, but it is also common among Coptic Christians, Americans and Koreans. The World Health Organization estimates that 30 pst. all men aged 15 and over are circumcised. ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e2059	The system of user-driven personal assistance – BPA – has as its primary objective freedom, seaworthiness and dignity for the individual. It is a system for those who have a need for help and, like others, have the opportunity to live as they wish. It is important for everyone to be the head of their own lives – as the case-rapporteur also said. Having a job, could take education, have a meaningful leisure time and be active in society is important in order to be able to be just that. A personal assistant – with whom the user is responsible and in charge – gives many disabled people the opportunity to live an active and committed life. Within a given framework, the user may control what the assistant is to do and the times at which the assistance is to be provided. BPA operates in such a way that the user, with the support of others, organises and manages the work of his assistants. It is important to stress that the municipalities already have a clear obligation to offer user-driven personal assistance. What we are adopting today is an individual right to BPA for users who meet defined requirements. Whether the government has hit the BPA in a good enough way, there are different opinions about. User organisations are moderately satisfied. The Labour Party believes that the right to BPA can be even more offensively designed, and we have a proposal in the vote on it. Significant work has been done in recent years to develop a system of rights-based BPA. The red-green parties intensified this post-election work in 2005, and the National Labour Party meeting in 2011 agreed to establish a right to BPA for users with a high level of assistance needs, and that costs should be investigated and discussed with the KS, user organisations and employee organisations. This work was the basis of a proposal for a right-holders' attachment submitted by the Ministry of Health and Health in the spring of 2013. I have noted that the two government parties in the comments on this matter are trying to present themselves as heroes, and the red-green parties are almost as opponents of the scheme. Firstly, that story is false, and secondly, it is an unusually easy way of dealing with a very important matter. I notice that the case rapporteur is dampening the rhetoric a little bit, but if we look at history and learn from it, it might be okay with a little reluctance. I would like to point out that all parties have acknowledged that the issue of the right-holding of BPA raises many issues, both in terms of scale, device, limitation and costs. It's sometimes given itself different expressions. For the treatment of Report. St. 40 for 2002–2003, Destruction of Disability Barriers, the Right and Christian People's Party was extremely sceptical about the rightful attachment of BPA and formulated the following: «The Committee's members from the Right and the Christian People's Party believe that legislating the right to BPA raises both fundamental and practical problems.» Here the two parties state that the scheme will have major distortive effects and that it violates the principle that it is the municipality to choose the form of assistance. In connection with the treatment of Report. St. 25 for 2005–2006, Mestring, opportunities and meaning, the Progress Party opposed the idea of an upper age limit for the scheme, while the current bourgeois government – including the Progress Party – now believes that users over 67 years of age should not be entitled to BPA. In other words, it is timely to remind you that all the parties, including the Workers' Party, have for periods of time packed to find the right arrangements for the BPA scheme. I think we should all now show a little humility, and make sure that the BPA system is as good as possible, for the benefit of the users. The Labour Party also sees BPA as part of a much wider effort, where, as a society, we need to have far stronger and more focused efforts towards individuals and groups that need extra community support and help. With this, I address the proposal that the Workers' Party is promoting alone and those that we are promoting together with SV in the setting, and that describes concrete improvements in the government's proposal for BPA.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e2089	The right-wing Party government aims to create the patient’s health system. We have a vision of a society where everyone can participate. It is this vision that is the basic plan of the government's health and welfare policy, and it is also reflected in the important matter we are now debating. User-controlled personal assistant, BPA, is an alternative way of organising services and personal assistance for people with disabilities, persons with a high need for assistance in their daily lives, both inside and outside the home. The rights attaching of BPAs shall contribute to facilitating their achievement, in line with others in society, of their objectives. A user of BPA has the role of head of labour and thus assumes responsibility for the organisation and content of the service, according to his own needs. In other words, the role of the manager gives users the necessary influence over their own life situation. For each user, the right to BPA will mean more predictability and a more active and independent life, despite its disability. The law-enforcement of BPA is simply a ‘exemptive tool’ that gives disabled people the same space for action for life as others, and the law is – to say it very carefully – far overtime. Already in 2004, the Stortinget asked the government to promote legislation on the right to user-led personal assistance. The Bondevik II government followed this up in 2005 in the state budget for 2006, without the follow-up of the government of Stoltenberg II, the red-green ones. Afterwards, a long fight was started to put in place a rights fixation of the BPA. This battle was specifically taken and supported by the Right Party, the Advance Party and the Christian People's Party here in the Storting, but it had to be a government shift to put in place today's law-enforcement. For that very reason, and because it has taken so long, the government chose to start from the model that has been out of consultation. This was because the proposal should have an opportunity to be dealt with by Stortinget before the holiday. It is therefore good that the proposal for a hearing fails to meet the government's ambitions sufficiently, and that the government has therefore chosen to meet the majority of hearing bodies and extended the right to include support contacts and relief for parents with children under 18 years of age with disabilities. To this end, the members of the Committee of the Right and the Advance Party expect user organisations to have their views and perspectives taken into account in the evaluation. In other words, the legalisation of BPA becomes a ‘exemptive tool’ that gives people with disabilities the same space for action for life as other citizens of the country. It's about predictability for people who already have a very tough life because of their disability. I dare say that this will be a win-win-thing for the whole community.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e2267	Several have expressed that this is a joyous day, and it is. It's a pleasure day for many disabled people who depend on assistance in everyday life. And it's a pleasure day for everyone who's been on for many years and struggled for the rights attaching of user-driven personal assistance, which we abbreviated as BPA. The majority decided in 2013 to ratify the UN Convention for People with Disabilities. The Convention provides significant support to disabled people in their struggle to achieve their rights, such as enabling disabled people to live as independent and dignified a life as possible. The Convention itself does not contain any new specific rights, but imposes specific requirements on how the Member States can best facilitate the benefit of universal human rights enshrined in the UN Conventions for the benefit of people with disabilities. The organisation is particularly concerned with the introduction of laws and regulations that prevent discrimination and enable disabled people to participate in all areas of society, just as we do in the Stortinget today, when we adopt legislation on user-led personal assistance. The government wants a society with opportunities for everyone, where everyone can participate. User-driven personal assistance is an important contribution to the objective of an active and as independent life for people with disabilities. The scheme implies that the person in need of assistance governs and organizes what concerns his or her own life. The role of the manager gives the user influence over his or her own life situation. Within the framework of agreed hours and working life rules, the user may control what the assistant is to do, at what times and where the assistance is to be provided. It's about having your hands on the wheel of your own life, an experience of freedom, independence and dignity that is important to all of us. An Econ study shows that BPA is also socio-economically profitable, among others. by providing user-driven personal assistance as the only means of providing disabled persons with assistance needs with opportunities for professional activity and social participation. About a quarter of the persons interviewed who have BPA report having regular work, about half of them. Half works 100 pst. Two thirds of those who are working believe that without BPA they would not be working. The rest believe that they must have fallen in job size. Family members would also be less economically active or had a lower job size if the disabled person with a need for assistance had not had BPA. The report shows that BPA leads to more disabled people receiving education. This in turn leads to increased professional activity and further socio-economic gains. The survey also shows that those with BPA schemes are much less in need of different types of health and care services, thus reducing the cost of traditional home services. Today's decision is an important principle decision and a good start to give disabled people with a need for assistance the same space for action as all of us.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e2388	I'm not going to wonder what other similarities the Knutsen representative has with the Virgin Mary, but I'm going to use the opportunity here to commend her work as the case-law rapporteur on this matter. It's been a pleasure working together in the committee, even though we didn't agree on everything. This issue is important for the further development of Norway and, in order to provide the best possible health care, healthcare professionals depend on patient information. Missing or incorrect information may lead to mistreatment, and to document what has been done, and on the basis of which the decision is taken, the information must be recorded – mainly for the benefit of the patient and the further treatment. This proposal means that today’s health record law is broken down into two new laws: the Patient Journal Act and the Health Register Act. Due to the professional and organisational development of healthcare, all parts of the Health Register Act are no longer functioning on the basis of intention. Since 2001, there have been major changes in the technical possibilities and expectations of the population for seamless exchange of information and access to their own health information. The right considers that several of the provisions of today’s law are prejudicial to a well-functioning patient course. In the past, a greater proportion of healthcare was provided within one establishment. However, there have been major changes in the course of patients. At present, each person has its health information disseminated between different systems in different establishments. Increased specialisation and interaction require a continuous involvement of several enterprises in the treatment of the same patient. Basic requirements for patient safety require a greater level of safe sharing of information between the enterprises involved. The changes in the law are designed to enable healthcare professionals to obtain relevant and necessary information in a timely and effective manner, irrespective of where the patient has received health care in the past. In short, rather than being transported by taxi, fax and pipe mail, this change in the law brings up electronic interaction in the healthcare system. We believe that electronic information exchange should not require specific consent requirements. The consent requirement should be the same regardless of whether the information is made available by means of paper-based information or by electronic means. Both legislative proposals have been amended on several points after the consultation. Part of the debate has been based on the consultation proposal and not on what is promoted in the legislative proposal. The new Patient Records Act provides better information sharing between health professionals than the current Health Register Act. It is statutory that the patient has the right to oppose making all or part of the record available to other health professionals. This can be done both by technical blocking and by verbal communication to patient caregivers. We are concerned that the rights should be well informed for the patient, and there are several comments about this in the setting. Privacy is ensured by general rules on professional secrecy. In any case, snoking is prohibited. With this law, Norwegian patients may manually refuse to have all or part of their records made available to other health professionals. It is important to clarify that the possibilities provided by the law cannot be put into effect until the necessary security solutions have been developed. In this case, the law goes ahead of the technology instead of running after it. Health professionals who want to make use of the possibilities provided by the law must first ensure that they are on the right level in terms of data security and technology. The availability and security of data must be ensured irrespective of where the patient has received health care and the organisation of the sector. This must be done within the framework of professional secrecy. The use of data shall continue to be regulated by the needs of patients throughout the course of treatment. These laws become maps and compasses in the further development of eHealth in Norway. We believe that today’s legislation prevents the development of the concept of one patient – one journal, an important piece that needs to be put in place in order to achieve the goals we have set for a future-oriented healthcare system on patients’ premises. The law confirms that the sharing of patient information shall only take place where necessary and relevant for patient treatment and other legal purposes. Unlawful access to and sharing of patient information is illegal and may result in criminal liability. This is also a consequence of the Health Care Act. Patients and users shall, as far as possible, have control of health information describing them. The patient should not contact more sites than is necessary to obtain a complete picture of his own data. Trust between patients and treating health professionals is a prerequisite for providing good quality health care and essential for providing the patient with sufficient information to receive proper and necessary health care. In the Hippocratic oath, among other things, it says:\t\t You can change the settings of the grid. \t\t \t ‘Everything that comes to my knowledge during the pursuit of my profession or in daily life with people, who should not be known to others, I will keep secret and never reveal.’ The obligation of professional secrecy of the healthcare system is one of the oldest and most important measures to ensure this trust.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e2482	The left is in favour of providing healthcare professionals with relevant and necessary information on their patients in a timely and effective manner. So it says that you have the right to oppose division, as if it were equivalent to an explicit consent. A right to oppose means that you have to act actively – you have to actually oppose sharing. First of all, it assumes that you know that the information can be shared, and second, that you know that you can oppose sharing. When will you be informed that you have the right to oppose division? Is it in the cordon of the bodyguard while the blood is spilling? Or is it the psychologist who in the first meeting with new patients will start the confidential conversation with a briefing that what is said could be passed on to an unknown number of health professionals across the country? In the case of express consent, it will automatically be informed that the record information can be shared, as part of the reasons why consent is sought. Only then will you really be able to decide if this is something you want, and only then can you have some control over the flow of information. A passive consent relieves the public of a high level of information liability, to which the Left is very sceptical. It is apparent from the legislative proposal that privacy has been protected and the Left is pleased that privacy has been included in the intended purpose. Is it possible, however, that privacy could be protected in an even better way than is the case in the proposals submitted? Andalucía in Spain, with its eight million inhabitants, has an IT infrastructure for healthcare that we can only dream about here to land. All hospitals and public medical centres have the same solution and can communicate electronically with each other without obstacles. This means that patient information actually follows the patient, so that all healthcare members have access to the same information when they are in contact with the patient. This also applies to the pharmacies supplying the patient’s medicinal products and providing advice on their use. In Andalucía, they have solved this by providing all citizens with a patient card for safe identification. There is no information in the card except that it identifies the patient. In order for healthcare professionals to have access to patient data, they generally need to have this patient card. Then the patient himself has full control over who gets access to their information, and they can give access to exactly what healthcare professionals they themselves want. No one can be suspected of misrepresenting sensitive personal data, and everyone has access to enough information to do the best possible job for the patient. It is possible to take into account effective communication while keeping individual control over their own sensitive information. One of the challenges of opening up cross-business sharing is that the journals are not structured. Journals are conducted differently and in many cases contain extensive information. It will therefore be a demanding process to look through a long record to find relevant information. Then you get unnecessary and extensive exposure of the many sensitive information that a journal may contain. There is a need to review both diagnostic descriptions and logging methods. The Left therefore proposes to appoint a LEAN Commission to review current journal practices and the training needs of healthcare professionals in order to establish a common standard for a digital journal. This applies regardless of the requirements for consent. Finally, I would like to say something about the proposal for a new Health Register Act. The left considers that a two-way sharing of the current law makes sense in order to clarify the difference between the use of record-keeping data in the context of treatment and for secondary purposes that does not directly benefit the patient concerned. Health records are important in research, production of statistics, analysis, planning, management and preparedness. We have a lot of good records, and that's how it still has to be. However, the left is critical that one of the proposed new health records provides for the creation of new registers by regulation. Finally, I would like to make the proposals that the Left alone or with others are in favour of.

What we see is a slightly unusual alliance of the Left on the bourgeois side and us and the Center Party on the red-green side, but unfortunately it's a common alliance on privacy issues. We're doing very well with the left there. The problem is that the data protection parties seem to be in chronic minority in this room.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e2526	The patient’s best — with better health care quality and improved patient safety — is the goal of everything we do in the health sector. In order to achieve this objective, it is essential to exploit the possibilities of effective and safe registration and sharing of patient information provided by modern ICT. The government’s proposal for a new patient record law and new health record law will open up the possibility for new, better and safer solutions to be developed and put into service. A consequence of increased specialisation and interaction is the ongoing involvement of several enterprises in the treatment of one and the same patient. This has created a new and greater need to share patient information that today’s legislation does not take into account. Too poor sharing of information in healthcare has for a long time led to poor patient treatment. The seamless exchange of information between health professionals is a prerequisite for the success of the reform work undertaken by this government in several areas. The Patient Journal Act will facilitate the availability of relevant and necessary information to healthcare professionals when they need it — in order to provide the patient with the best possible health care. The journal shall be linked to the patient, not to the activity in which the patient has received health care in the past. The few people want to share everything with everyone. We do not want unauthorised persons to have access to health information about ourselves. But we don't get good privacy by making it difficult to share information between people who need it, in order to provide the best possible health care. We do not propose that more patient information be shared. What's new is how the information can be shared. If a hospital or permanent medical practitioner has modern electronic patient records with high safety in the sharing of information, they may choose to provide health professionals with direct access to information necessary to provide the patient with health care elsewhere. Only those who need it will see and use the information, and only in connection with the patient’s seeking health care, or to administer it. The patient should be entitled, as at present, to see who has seen the information and to oppose its sharing with other treatment providers. Of course, the obligation of professional secrecy applies. The Patient Journal Act will be one of many steps towards the government’s vision of ‘one resident — one journal’. Here we have several parallel processes, including: on the standardisation, structuring and development of new and better record-keeping systems. Once we have developed the systems and carefully evaluated different solutions, we will present a new proposal for a single national journal solution that gathers all the patient’s health information. We need health data to treat each patient, but also to get more knowledge of causes and occurrence of diseases, how they are investigated and treated, and what kind of results different treatment methods can provide. The new Health Register Act will provide easier and more efficient access to and use of health data to obtain that type of knowledge. The law opens the door to the establishment of health records where individuals may object to their inclusion in the register. We will then be able to gather useful knowledge while respecting the individual's right to self-determination. However, that type of register can only be established if the register is important and it is not possible to base the register on individual consent. The representative Tove Karoline Knutsen referred to some of my views in this kind of question some years ago. It's absolutely right that in this kind of question, both I and Right have moved us. However, in the context of Stortinget’s treatment of the Stortingen Storingsmessageen One incorporates – one journal and in the context of the nuclear record case, one found that Stortinget had a different attitude to this kind of question than it had in the pre-enlargement period. There is today reason to thank former Minister of Health Anne-Grete Ström-Erichsen for having started an important reform work in this area. The new Patient Records Act and the Health Register Act will open up new and safer solutions to improve health care for individual patients and to develop healthcare for the benefit of the patient. Then it's important that we take the privacy discourse from the paper society to the digital society and see the opportunities for enhanced privacy that increased, new digitisation involves.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e2645	I want to address the discussion related to what is very often said, namely that because we now have electronic journals and ICT systems, we have easier access than with paper, and it's just a keystroke away. It's easy to say, but if you think a little bit through the reality, I think it might be a little bit different. When one had paper records, health professionals who had key to the room where the paper records were – if one was so lucky that it was locked – could read these journals completely without any kind of control. The only way to control was to ‘freshen’. Today you have a full overview of who is in the journals. We as patients also have a completely different way of controlling whether, for example. the neighbour, if his neighbour works in the health care system, is in and reads the file. That, I think, has a significantly greater effect in avoiding that kind of twisting. It's not that it's just the parties that have other suggestions in this room, that are concerned with privacy. Privacy is well protected in this legislative proposal. There are enhanced privacy measures in relation to what was sent at the hearing. The applicable consent rules shall be continued. This means that, in normal cases, healthcare professionals will obtain information from other establishments in consultation with the patient. To make specific demands for consent beyond this will make information exchange in a treatment situation more difficult. Healthcare professionals already have the right and obligation to share information, but the obligation of professional secrecy does not change. What is new in the proposal is the way to share the information. If e.g. a hospital or permanent staff member has good and modern systems, they may choose to provide health professionals with direct access to the record according to the proposed legislation. Health professionals may themselves seek relevant information about the patient, but hospitals or medical clinics that do not ensure the patient’s privacy in a good way in their systems cannot provide other health professionals with access and the patient will still be able to refuse this type of information. The legislative proposal does not mean that all healthcare professionals will have access to all information about a patient. Only healthcare professionals need to provide the patient with health care, who can see the information, and they should only see the information necessary for health care. There is therefore a difference between what the prescriber, the hospital doctor and the physiotherapist will be able to read. It is important that we maintain a high level of privacy in a modern system, because I fully agree with all those who have said that healthcare depends on people having confidence that the information about them is well managed.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e2692	The size should now, far by day, when everyone starts to get a little warm and weary, deal with a matter that in itself is quite complicated, because it contains a number of proposals for changes in many laws. In addition, it has a title that does not immediately tell the unknown what the matter is really about, in all its facets. Nor is it made easier by the fact that there are varying majority and minorities in the Committee for and against government's various legislative proposals. Moreover, neither the Christian People's Party nor the Green Party are represented in the Committee, so when we come as far as voting on the matter at some point in the night, it is a matter of having a heavy tongue in the mouth, while at the same time imposing a great responsibility on the prisoners that the votants actually reflect what is the view of the majority in the matter. As rapporteur of the case, it is my responsibility here and now to try to drag us through the main points of the case, in a way that makes the Storting have an overview of what we are going to decide on. But before I do, I, as the rapporteur-in-Office, have a small appeal to the Council of State, which I hope he will take up and at least reflect on, in view of future matters. I have been a major representative for nine years and the case rapporteur for many cases, but I have never bothered more to get an overview of the content of a case than I have done with this proposition. The title may indicate, at first sight, that this is a technical adaptation to Decision No. Stortinget has already made, in the case of a new disability benefit scheme and a new disability pension scheme for public occupational pension schemes, but it is not that simple. The proposals include amendments to a number of laws – the Swedish Law on Social Security, the Law on Social Security for New Disembarkation, which will enter into force only on 1 January 2015, the Act on the Act on the Retirement of War for Military Persons, the Act on the Retirement of Civil Servants, the Law on Retirement of Seafarers, the Act on the Pension of the State Pensions Act, the Act on the Pension of Pharmacies, the Act on Damage to War, the Fischerpension Act, the Act on Occupational Injury, the Act on Nursing Pensions, etc. Some of these are purely technical adaptations to new disability benefits, which are necessary adaptations to new disability benefits and to a new disability pension scheme in public service pensions, but which can be made in slightly different ways and where selected adaptations are relevant to future benefits. Some of the proposals are not adaptations to the new scheme, but proposals for amendments to the Stortinget Decision have already been made. Some proposals deal with a number of benefits and have really no connection with the implementation of new disability benefits and new public disability pension schemes and could, as such, have been promoted in a separate proposition, where the title had not been about disability benefits or disability pensions at all. In addition, a split is proposed in one of the paragraphs of the Amending Act of the People's Rights Act, which makes the paragraphs of some of the amendments out of line with the paragraphs of the proposed law. When the committee was going to consider proposals for progress in the matter, one of the questions we were going to consider was whether we should have a hearing on the matter. We initially thought that maybe that wasn't necessary. We then referred to the title of the case. I think most people immediately perceived this as a matter that only contained the necessary adaptations to previous decisions, but we decided to hold hearings. Only one organisation signed up, the National Public Pensioners Agency. I have a suspicion that this may perhaps be due to the fact that the few – like us in the committee – are able to read out of the title of the case what the matter really involved. If that is the case, it is a democratic problem that we should reflect on, whether we are in position or in opposition. Then comes my little recommendation to the Council of State, because former Councillor Rigmor Aasrud started a project – Clear language in the state – that the current Minister of Modernization Jan Tore Sanner has pressed to his chest. Prop. 66 L for 2013–2014 had hardly stood out in this perspective. As elected representatives, we will address important questions for our citizens, and a minimum is then that we understand what the matter is. Clear language is particularly important when we have a short time to deal with the matter, as in this case. It's an old word that says that in many cases it might be wise to keep your mouth shut and let others think you're stupid, instead of opening your mouth and raising any doubt. When in this case I dare to spite this wise counsel and actually open my mouth, it is partly because in my former life, as I said, I have been the case rapporteur for many matters, but that I have also been the caseworker of the social security department of the then Social Affairs Department, and I have been a researcher on disability in the social security research group at the Institute for Applied Social Science Research. But I had problems getting through the text of this proposition. So my request to the Council of State is therefore that he critically assesses whether it is possible to simplify the language of future propositions on such complex issues. Back to the case, which I choose to review based on politics and not engineering. As a guide, I have chosen to divide the proposals into four main groups. The first consists of perfectly sound adaptations, either of a technical nature, or of which there is broad political agreement, both between the parties and among the hearing bodies which have given their opinion not in spring but to the ministry's hearing in the matter. I simply do not choose to spend time here, except for one, which concerns the lifting of the provision on disability for home-worker spouses. This means that the few – in practice women – are to be assessed hereafter in relation to their future labour market opportunities, which would give them a higher degree of disability than if they were to be assessed in relation to the possibilities of housework. Everyone agrees. The second group is a proposal for the necessary adaptations to the rules already adopted, but where different solutions are possible and where there are different opinions among the hearing bodies. This applies, inter alia, to: the various proposals for changes to the coordination law, the design of a temporary supplement to the disability benefit pending new, permanent rules and new, simpler rules for redeployment where the benefit paid has been too low or too high. An overall committee has here – despite different views among the stakeholders – chosen to support the government’s proposals. A third main group of proposals relates to new policies which the Stortinget has not previously taken up and on which there is a partial political disagreement. This concerns the reduction of the benefits of popular protection while staying in an institution under state responsibility and in a prison under criminal law. The benefits that have the greatest changes are new and old disability benefits and disability benefits, new and old old old-age pensions, former family care, surviving spouses and child pensions. A Joint Committee supports the harmonisation of legislation for all these performances. In addition, the rules need to be amended where they relate to the concepts of basic pension and supplementary pension, which will gradually disappear. A Joint Committee also supports the new rules on the date of conversion during residence in an institution, which implies that the conversion is carried out in the fourth month following the date of the deposit. In addition, an overall committee supports the government's proposal to continue the current rules on the non-reduction of benefits by enrolment in somatic hospital wards. However, the minority of the Committee, the Labour Party, the Centre Party and the SV have, in minority proposal No. 3 asked the government to investigate the consequences of total or partial non-discrimination between patients in somatic hospital wards and patients admitted to psychiatric wards. The minority here supports Mental Health's observation that today's practice may be discriminatory, but at the same time sees that there may be reasons why the two patient groups cannot have exactly the same rules. Therefore, the minority’s proposal is that the matter be investigated before a final decision is taken on the question. Where there is a difference of view between majority and minority, the government's proposal for a new rule is that the benefits of former family nurses, surviving spouses, child pensions, new disability benefits and old age pensions fall entirely under criminal law in one of the prisons of criminal law. The minority of the Committee, the Labour Party, the Centre Party and the SV, believe that this is going too far, because these benefits have been earned on a basis that has nothing to do with the criminal acts that have been committed, and recommends that the Storting vote against these proposals. In the opinion of the minority, they should also be equated with residence in an institution. The minority have also noticed that a similar rule is not proposed for war pensioners and their children and any survivors. On the other hand, the majority of the Committee, the Right, the Progressive Party and the Left, support the Government's proposal that pensioners and professionals should be treated as equivalent in criminal proceedings. In order to make the suspension of the case as simple as possible around this point, the Committee asks that the vote in this part of the case be held in such a way that § 16-12, concerning benefits to former family nurses in criminal proceedings, be voted over before § 16-11, on equivalent benefits in institution, and in the same way for surviving spouses § 17-14 before § 17-13, for child pensions § 18-9 before § 18-8, for old-age pensions § § 19-22 and 20-23 before, respectively, § 19-21 and § 20-22, and for disability benefits § 12-20 before § 12-19. The easiest thing would be to take all of these paragraphs on loss of performance during criminal proceedings in one vote before voting on the remainder of Roman number XIV in the election. Then the fourth main group of proposals remains, sorted by policy: proposals for which the government is now proposing a rematch on parts of the collapse decision from the previous period, on new disability benefits and old-age pensions to disability. These are decisions which have entered into force on the one hand and which will enter into force on the other hand on 1 January 2015. It was a political dispute about them in the last period, and it is a political dispute about them now. The decision of the previous period which has already entered into force concerns the decision to partially protect against the new age adjustment in the old-age pension. The decision in the previous period provides half-screening until 2018 and involves life-long screening for unborn born 1944-1951. The reason was that the disabled do not have the same opportunity to adapt to the standard of living as the workers have, while we were lacking, and lacked, evidence that could show how the actual adaptation of the workers would be. The Decision assumes that a reassessment of the continuation of the screening determination will be carried out in 2018. The Workers' Party, SV, the Centre Party, the Christian People's Party and the Left voted for this protection provision in the previous period. The government is now proposing to remove this provision for premature birth from 1948 onwards. The majority of the Committee, the Labour Party, the Centre Party, the Left and the SV, have not endorsed the Government's proposal to remove this safeguard clause for failure. Therefore, the Government's proposal for amendments to paragraphs 19-9(a) of the People's Rights Act has been withdrawn from the committee position and is available instead as minority proposals from the Right and the Advance Party. The same applies to the Government’s proposal to amend the new provisions for determining earnings before disability, which were adopted in the previous period and which have not yet entered into force but are therefore due to enter into force on 1 January 2015. This income – before disability – is used to determine the degree of disability. The discount agreed in the previous period that pre-employment income should be determined on the basis of income at the default date/year before the default date. The Government is now proposing to amend the Decision, so that it is the average of the three best of the last five years on which to base the determination of earnings before disability. This option was discussed in the previous period and rejected due to adverse effects on persons with income increases shortly before the default date. Then all the lots, except the Advance Party, were behind the decision that was taken. The majority in today's committee - the Working Party, the Centre Party, the Left and the SV - is on the last decision and has taken this point out of the setting. It is now available as part of minority motion No. 1 from the Right and Advance Party. Both the decision taken in the previous period and the Government's proposal in the present case require certain exceptions to the general rule that legislation should not become too rigid. The exceptions are, of course, quite different – a bit with the opposite sign – since the last decision and the government’s proposals are now different. As a result of the rule already adopted, the majority of the Committee has returned to the vote, while the exceptions proposed by the government, which logically have to be rejected by the majority in the present case, are set out in minority motions No. 1 from the Right and Advance Party. The last proposal, in which the Government's proposal has not received a majority in the Committee, is the question of differentiated rates of maximum reduction for losses with minimum benefits, where the Storting has adopted 3.3 G for married/cohabitant and 3.5 G for single.
