ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e2944	Prop. 73 L from the government contains some proposed changes to holiday laws. These are the results of the EU Working Time Directive, which is incorporated into the EEA Agreement, including: the requirement for employees to receive annual paid leave of at least four weeks is laid down. This directive has been implemented by Norway, and it has been in force here for many years. In recent years, however, the European Court of Justice has passed several judgments on Article 7 of the Directive, making it necessary to amend the Norwegian holiday law. With the clarifications given by the Court of Justice, it turns out that one of our systems is in breach of the Directive, for example: is the condition in the current Norwegian law of at least six days' work incapacity for leave, not in line with Article 7(b) of the Working Time Directive. 1st@item: inlistbox The European Court of Justice is aware that the right to four weeks paid leave every year is absolute, and employees shall not lose this right in the event of sickness, whether it occurs in advance or during the holidays. This suggests that the Committee is a good clarification from the EU. The whole committee is of the opinion that amending holiday laws § 9 paragraph. The second paragraph will strengthen the right of workers to leave and, to an even greater extent, to support what is the purpose of the holiday laws and the intention of rescuing and rescuing workers. The government's proposal goes well beyond what the directive requires, and some employers' organisations believe that the lack of special regulation for statutory leave beyond the requirements of the directive means an unnecessary over-fulfilment of the directive. To that end, I would like to stress that it is important not to create more complicated legislation than is strictly necessary, and the Committee therefore supports the government's position not to introduce specific rules for different types of statutory leave. The other thing that has been concluded is that today's holiday laws provide more opportunities for holiday money to be paid than is the case under Article 7 of the Working Time Directive. ESA has concluded that this is contrary to this article. The Committee is therefore required to agree with the government that the possibility of receiving holiday pay rather than holiday leave must fall, even in the event of sickness or parental leave. Here, too, it is important to have a simple and prudent regulatory framework. The Committee therefore does not wish to propose special rules for the payment of holiday pay for statutory holidays in addition to the requirements of the Directive. Then there has been a discussion in the committee as to whether it may be unfortunate for employees to promise to come into force from 1 July, mid-calendar. The majority of the Committee recommends that a team of 1 July 2014 as the time for the entry into force of the Act. The reason for this is that the proposed changes, as we see them, will strengthen the right of workers to leave in the event of illness. There'll be an advantage for these people if from July 1st this year, if they get sick, they'll be able to have their holidays postponed. In addition, today's rules are contrary to the Working Time Directive, and we thereby breach our obligations under the EEA Agreement. This strongly argues that the rules should be amended as soon as advice is given, in order to ensure that Norwegian legislation complies with the Directive. This applies even if the proposal could have some economic consequences for employers and the public. The other changes and proposals that this aggregation proposition makes up, are the deal in the committee, so to save time on a long meeting day, I won't comment on the other.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e3186	Today, we are discussing changes to the law and follow-up to the revised IA agreement agreed by the parties in March of this year. The objectives of reducing sick leave, including the inclusion of more people with reduced working capacity in the labour market and of stimulating people to stay longer in work are set. However, the new agreement contains all necessary simplifications in the follow-up to the IA agreement of 2010. The whole basic idea of the IA agreement is that work is often the right medicine, that being able to go to work little by little, being able to have contact with the working life, even when you're ill, can help get you back faster. Work creates health. You don't have to go to work only when you're healthy. You should also be able to go to work to get well. This is the employer’s side, the employer’s side and the Norwegian authorities’ agreement. It also suggests that the IA agreement is perhaps the best example of the good results that the Norwegian model and tripartite cooperation provide to the Norwegian community. Reforms come into being in agreement, thereby also increasing the possibilities for good intentions to become reality. Very few other countries can refer to the type of obligatory, long-term and involved work. The IA agreement of 2010 gave us – after extensive work in different expert groups and after negotiations between the parties – extensive changes in the follow-up to sick leave, including obligations and monitoring of follow-up and penalties for non-response. As the Storting then, in 2010, considered the necessary legislative changes in order to implement that agreement, an overall committee stressed that sanctions should never be an end in themselves but that they could be an appropriate instrument, but only if they actually lead to a change in behaviour and better follow-up. The Committee then asked that this be followed closely. We also quickly saw that parts of the 2010 IA agreement were more difficult to implement and follow-up than planned, including the sanctioning regime that was released in time. In January 2012, I addressed an interpellation to the Minister of Labour at the time, so that the Storting could be properly briefed, but also an opportunity to discuss how the IA agreement was implemented, how the new instruments worked. We just did that because we saw – through various headlines in the media and also through different recordings we got around at the workplace – that there were opportunities for improvement. I am very pleased that the new IA agreement and the changes in the laws we are adopting today are doing exactly what we then needed, that the IA work is improved by simplifying and red tape of the sick leave follow-up. We're continuing what works today, reverses what doesn't work and improves the agreement on a number of points. The IA agreement will never lead to a more inclusive working life for more people if it is not actively followed up by the parties. We are totally dependent on the fact that the different actors want to have the agreement and thus carry out what is their part of the work. We must have employees, employers and health professionals who believe in the contract, who want the contract, and who play on the team. In addition, it is extremely important for the future of the agreement that it appears lucrative to the companies – that it is worth it – and not just leads to burdensome desktop routines. In my opinion, it is important that the Storting also discuss the implementation and status of the IA work at regular intervals, so that we can be well up-to-date, so that we can make it work as planned, and that we can also help to make implementation easier if necessary. It is also important that the Storting discuss inclusive working life at regular intervals, because we also know that debate and attention around, for example. sick leave, in itself, helps to reduce absenteeism. Finally, I would just like to point out that an overall committee highlights the importance of the human resources now available within Nav, which will no longer operate with control and follow-up in that way, instead – and of course – used for closer and better follow-up of sick people in other ways.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e3207	When Prop. 102 L is presented to the Stortinget, it is about changes to the Labour Environment Act and the National Social Security Act, about simplifying the legislation for the follow-up of sick workers. The government's aim is to get more people working because work has a self-value for the individual, but also for society. Work means that each contributes to building Norway stone by stone. At the same time, there are also people who are part of the ordinary working life, who can face obstacles that make them sick later. It is a challenge for the sick person, but also for the employer – for the sick person because you fall out of the labour market completely or for a period of time, but also for the employer because important skills are lost in part or completely in the same period. That is why the IA Agreement, which was concluded on 4 March of this year, is so important because it is designed to provide a good working environment at work, but also to actively remove the obstacles that make people sick. The IA Agreement has a background to a tradition of cooperation and trust between authorities, workers and employers in Norway. The agreement is based on a common recognition that the workplace is the most important containment area and that early activation of sick-seekers and the implementation of organisational measures at the workplace will help prevent and reduce sick leave. The most important thing is interaction in the workplace, not forms and penalties. The aim is to build trust between the employer and the sick person. Simplification and less red tape in the follow-up to sick workers is also the government's objective to free resources both in the workplace and in the Employment and Welfare Act. The aim is lower sick leave, higher retirement age and increased integration of people with disabilities into the labour market.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e3264	There is a great deal of agreement on the government’s proposals for tax, tax and customs legislation in relation to the revised national budget for 2014 and I would assume that other parties will explain their divergent views in the areas where this is relevant. There is a broad consensus on the proposed action relating to petroleum taxes and transitional rules for the reduction of free income. This amendment is an adjustment to the decision taken by Stortinget in connection with the revised national budget last year, where the rate of exemption was reduced, but where it was arranged for a transitional arrangement in which this reduced rate of freedom was not to apply to projects subject to plans for expansion or application under the petroleum Act § 4-2 and § 4-3, delivered to the Ministry of Oil and Energy before 5 May 2013. The Government's proposal in this proposition to extend the transitional rule will apply where there is a necessary link between the development and pipeline project, so that the overall project is also covered by the transitional arrangement. Furthermore, a time limit has also been set within which the transitional rules will actually apply, and the majority of them agree that the rule does not affect costs incurred as of the 2021 year of income. It is good that the majority supports this change. One of our main competitive advantages for the oil and gas activity on the Norwegian base is stability and predictability related to the framework conditions. This adjustment strengthens the predictability of investment decisions on Norwegian pedestal. Furthermore, it is a unanimous committee that supports under the Government's proposals for changes to the shipping tax system and joint and several liability for employers' obligations here, and also agrees on the amendments proposed in relation to VAT and real estate. In the case of changes proposed in the boat engine fee, the tax-free scheme and the electricity charge, these have been dealt with in the Innst. 260 S for 2013–2014 and will be fully debated in the context of the review of the revised national budget in the Grand Duchy next Friday. The Government is committed to ensuring the legal certainty of taxpayers, and this is also a field of action enshrined in the Sundvolden Declaration. In this round, the government proposes, among other things: tightening the rules on public tax lists. The tax lists shall remain public on the tax haven’s websites on the Internet, but in such a way that information on who has applied for in the tax lists is made available electronically to taxpayers. Similarly, taxpayers will have access to information about who has been provided with written tax list information, in addition to spouses, roommates, bankruptcies or courts. There is a broad consensus that information on shareholders in limited liability companies should be publicly available. All limited liability companies are also required to have a shareholder book under Section 4-5 of the Share Act, and this shareholder book shall be available to anyone who wishes to have access to it. In addition, there has been a discussion relating to the Tax DG’s shareholder register and the disclosure of shareholders and the provision of information. It is such that this shareholder register only contains information about the company until the end of the year for which it is reported, and that register is therefore less suitable for being some kind of digital platform or public-friendly solution for collecting shareholder information. The Right and Advance Parties, together with a Joint Committee, want to facilitate a greater degree of available information on who had to own shares in Norwegian companies at all times. Therefore, the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Fisheries, together with the Ministry of Finance, is asked to establish a public solution with information on shareholders, which ensures greater transparency, with establishment during 2015. We look forward to this establishment and the contribution that this will make information about ownership more accessible.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e3325	It's a unanimous finance committee that stands together about Prop's setting. 87 L. I would like to thank the Committee for the cooperation which has been delegated to the Committee in the preparation of the matter. There is no doubt that the Norwegian financial industry is important for Norway. The financial industry provides capital to enterprises, distributes risks and contributes to the building up of capital in society. The financial industry is international, and we therefore need international rules to facilitate access by Norwegian companies to the EU and the rest of the world. The proposal for a new law on credit rating agencies comes, for example: as a consequence of EU Regulation No. 1060/2009 on credit rating agencies. The new law is important because credit rating agencies play an increasingly important role when more and more Norwegian companies borrow money for growth and new jobs directly in the bond market rather than through traditional banks. The regulation of outsourcing is a central moment in the suspension. A co-opted committee stresses that consideration of the sound organisation under the concession, privacy and unimpaired supervisory authority of the undertaking must be included in the overall assessment. The customer owns his own information and the protection of privacy and confidentiality of customer information is important. A co-ordinated financial committee also underlines the need to take efficiency and competitiveness into account at the same time. The aim of the law is, of course, not to impose unnecessary competitive disadvantages on Norwegian financial undertakings compared to competitors in other countries. The aim is to ensure sound regulation of outsourcing also in Norway. Norway is bound by EU rules and, of course, does not want to go beyond these rules. Another important theme in the proposition is gender-neutral contributions and benefits in life insurance contracts that are private, voluntary and out of employment. Today, the requirement of coherence between premium and risk means that insurance companies cannot ignore factors that have a significant impact on the risk. This means that women have higher pension contributions than men because women have longer life expectancy. Now the law is amended so that insurance companies can no longer use gender as a calculation criterion. However, this change creates a new issue related to the transfer of existing contracts with guaranteed benefits and profit-sharing. Any requirement for relocation to trigger gender-neutral tariffs in the company to which the contract is transferred would lead to the relocation of the traditional, guaranteed contracts that IPA and annuity have stopped. I am therefore pleased that a joint finance committee supports the government's proposal that the transfer of such agreements cannot be regarded as the conclusion of a new agreement and that companies must be given sufficient time to adapt to new rules. This is about finding practical and good solutions that do not affect the industry more than is strictly necessary. In conclusion, let me once again thank the Committee for its good and constructive cooperation.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e3347	I Prop. 87 L many different legislative proposals have been promoted in several financial market laws. I note that the Committee has unanimously joined the proposals. Several of the legislative changes are follow-up to the EEA Agreement. The current rules on the ownership of stock exchanges and securities registers are changed to owner-control rules, similar to those currently applicable, inter alia. financial institutions and investment firms. With these changes, a long-term discussion with ESA could hopefully end. Requirements for gender-neutral contributions and benefits are introduced for new contracts in non-employment private and voluntary life insurance. In order to contribute to a functioning relocation market for savings and pension agreements with guaranteed benefits and profit-sharing which are no longer offered in the market, it is considered that the transfer of such contracts should not be considered as the conclusion of a new agreement. I have noticed that a joint committee supports this. I have also noticed that the Committee agrees with the government that companies need to be given sufficient time to adapt to new rules, and it is proposed that the rules enter into force on 1 January 2015. The rules of the Financial Contracts Act implementing the EEA rules on cross-border payments in euro and technical requirements for credit transfers and direct debits in euro are updated in line with new EU and EEA rules. A separate new law on credit rating agencies implements EEA rules corresponding to EU Regulation No. 1060/2009, CRA I, on credit rating agencies. The Regulation was adopted in the EU before the adoption of the new EU system of financial supervision, and therefore no adaptations were necessary when incorporated into the EEA Agreement. However, following CRA I, both CRA II and CRA III have been adopted in the EU. Those amending Regulations confer on ESMA supranational competence, which is the EU Securities Markets Authority, and therefore cannot be incorporated into the EEA Agreement without adaptation. It will not be possible to establish and operate credit rating activities in the EEA territory without also subjecting the activities to the requirements of CRA II and CRA III. It will therefore not be possible to establish this type of activity in Norway until now, despite the new law now adopted. The implementation of the Regulation in Norwegian law is therefore made solely to comply with our formal EEA obligations. Furthermore, the right to the processing of sensitive personal data in connection with authorisation schemes for employees is now regulated. investment firms, unit trusts and financial institutions. This applies to private schemes without any official approval. Authorisation schemes are a positive complement to the regulation of public law, inter alia. financial and insurance advisers. These schemes contribute to increased knowledge and competence among these professional groups, which will benefit consumers. I therefore believe that it has been important to facilitate the provision of adequate access to sensitive personal data to these systems, so that they can function properly and at the same time be subject to adequate supervision by the Data Protection Authority. The outsourcing of business by financial institutions is an important – and growing – field, which at the same time appears to be weakly regulated. Now rules are adopted that better clarify the regulation of outsourcing in Norwegian legislation. There is no contradiction between the smallest standards laid down by the EU, the national space for action provided for by the EU rules, and the legal provisions we are now laying down in Norway. The rules are based on proposed new rules on outsourcing in the Banking Law Commission’s suspension with draft new Financial Enterprise Law. The legal rules adopted today will, in the same way as recently adopted financial market legislation, be incorporated into the proposal for a new single law on financial undertakings and the financial group that will be promoted in a short time. Because outsourcing is an important area, through this proposition, we have asked for the Storting's immediate consideration of the proposal for rules. As stated in the proposal and in the setting, competitive conditions will be considered as part of the overall picture to be taken when the concession clause is to be exercised. The aim of the law is not to impose unnecessary competitive disadvantages on Norwegian financial undertakings vis-à-vis undertakings in other countries, but to provide for proper regulation of outsourcing also in Norway. There will be a significant possibility for different types of outsourcing under the proposal. Non-core tasks may in principle be contracted if they are sound and the outsourced activities can be supervised. Core tasks may not initially be outsourced unless such outsourcing is permitted by law. A judgement will be made in relation to applications or outsourcing notices when deciding on the issue of authorisation. The starting point is that there are major challenges and potential risks associated with outsourcing, depending on the nature of the individual outsourcing and the manner in which it is intended. It is important to ensure that legitimate and important supervisory aspects are taken into account, as well as ensuring proper operation. It is also important to ensure that there is no uncertainty about the safe and confidential processing of personal data and more generally confidential information. Such information may have significant value for criminals, may be difficult to repair when the damage has occurred, and may also have great value for commercial purposes with which the customer is not familiar or familiar. So there are major societal interests in ensuring that this is a field that is subject to appropriate legal regulation. Proposals have also been made for the amendment of Section 7 of the Financial Supervision Act, which is a clean-up and simplification of the provision.

There can certainly be opportunities here to deal with maritime safety in a more coordinated and appropriate way as well as a potential for efficiency improvement in better coordination. With regard to the geographical scope of the unemployment obligation, an overall committee concludes that unemployment obligations within the baseline are mainly maintained. However, the Christian People's Party does not agree that the disembarkation field in the Oslo soil is being moved to the Baskøj area. We have noted the strong motivations of several hearings to move it into the district of Baskøll, as proposed by the ministry. Safety-related important hearing bodies are weakened by the move in this highly vulnerable area. The consideration of the environment and people is best cared for, we believe, by not moving it from where it is today. On the part of the Christian People's Party, we are otherwise satisfied with the flexibility of the fairway system. This contributes to a reduction in the cost of maritime transport. We are committed to achieving measures that can reduce costs – that is good. In this respect, we also welcome the announced reduction of the unloading charges in the future budget – a contribution together with other instruments to be able to transfer more goods from country to sea. The Christian People's Party fully supports the steps taken by the government to separate the operational part of the unloading service from the rest of the service by adding it to its own unit directly under the head office of the Coastal Works. Today’s model prevents the service from being efficient enough and has created long and sometimes unclear decision-making lines. There is an advantage in creating a greater distance between management and operational operations, and the roles are thus also more clearly defined. However, we will be concerned that this must not mean that all the loosing activity is collected in one place. We fear a centralization trend that is also not useful for the peculiarity of the activity. It should be a central task to seek to maintain a professional and competence environment along our entire coast. In the case of pilotage, it must be seen in conjunction with the organisation of the operational pilotage service. This must not lead to the pooling of pilots into one office, but to the finding of the most efficient and functional organisation according to the needs of the users. The utility service is very important for a good and efficient service. From the Christian People's Party we are positive to the announced competitive outsourcing of the accommodation service. However, it is important to impose high requirements on the design and capacity of pilots and on the competence of pilots. Nor must the requirement for the most reasonable operation be at the expense of life and health. In the current assessment concerning the organisation of maritime traffic centres, it is important that different alternatives can be identified. We want to await the investigation, but we want to show that future reorganization must take care of security and preparedness in the best possible way. Therefore, it is not certain that the outcome of this assessment should be that all seven today are merged into one. I refer you to the rapporteur's excellent review.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e3912	The matter under consideration today proposes amendments to the Law of 21 June 2002 No. 45 on professional transport by motor vehicle and vessel — the Professional Transport Act. The proposal facilitates the implementation of the EEA rules on bus and coach passenger rights. The EU regulation on bus passenger rights sets minimum requirements for the rights of bus passengers. It imposes obligations on Member States, carriers, bus terminal operators, travel agents and tour operators. The aim is to improve passenger rights in the area of bus and coach transport with a specific aim of improving the rights of disabled persons or persons with reduced mobility. This Regulation shall ensure non-discrimination in relation to the transport conditions offered by carriers, in addition to providing assistance to disabled persons or persons with reduced mobility. This means, among other things: a carrier cannot refuse to board a passenger due to disability or reduced mobility. Furthermore, it means that disabled persons or persons with reduced mobility are entitled to make reservations and purchase tickets at no additional cost. Furthermore, the rights of passengers in the event of accidents and the rules on liability and obligations of carriers in the event of cancellation and delay. It also has general rules on the handling and enforcement of complaints. The hearing bodies in the case have expressed a great deal of positive opinion on the proposal to extend the rights of bus passengers. The Committee is committed to strengthening the rights of a bus passenger in general by making the EU Regulation part of Norwegian law. This applies in particular to the rights of disabled persons. A Joint Committee supports the intention of the Government to establish a national complaint mechanism for bus passengers by extending the existing Air and Rail Passengers Transport Complaint to include the handling of complaints by bus passengers. The Committee also welcomes the fact that the Transport Complaint is able to deal with cases by those travelling by tram, tram and urban railway. A common complaint subject should be considered for all public transport. The Committee points out that the Regulation imposes on terminal service providers and carriers measures to facilitate the travel of disabled customers or persons with reduced mobility and that the measures apply to journeys over 250 km. However, the Committee sees that the need for assistance services can indeed be equally great in short trips, and therefore we ask the Ministry to provide an overview of how this has been adequately addressed locally. The Committee also asks the Ministry to consider how to ensure, where appropriate, the future organisation and financing of assistance services at terminals, which also include internal public transport, where there are currently absolutely most travellers. From a minority in the Committee, it is noted that the working conditions of professional drivers should not be adversely affected by the new legislation and that the safety and working environment of drivers should be ensured in the context of the legislative changes and in the preparation of the regulations. There is reason to rejoice that through this amendment, we have not only secured passenger rights in general in a better way, but in particular improved the rights of disabled persons or persons with reduced mobility. This is a group that is protected by this amendment in a much better way than before. There have been too many people in our country who have been rejected or otherwise prevented from using public collective services because of their disability. I am therefore pleased that today we have taken a long step on that road that guarantees all groups of people the same rights as passengers. An overall committee recommends the proposed amendments to the Professional Transport Act.

This concerns passengers travelling on the long bus journeys, but it is also pointed out that although local services such as buses and trams and other public transport are not included locally, we have our own laws that in many ways respect the rights granted by this regulation for the long journeys. It has to be implemented, and it has to be done as soon as possible. ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e4008	On behalf of the President-in-Office of the Council, I would like to thank the Cooperation Committee. Prop. 64 L is a relatively complicated proposition, which we have had relatively short time on in the committee. Ensuring consumer rights has been and is important. Everyone has ever come into a situation where a product was not as it was manufactured in the marketing, or purchased a product that does not work, or entered into a contract that was not complied with. It is precisely for this reason that it is important that consumer rights are guaranteed by law. The purpose of the Directive is, inter alia, to: facilitating cross-border trade. Member States shall have uniform rules as regards certain aspects of contracts concluded between consumers and traders. It is also an objective to achieve a high level of consumer protection. This Directive covers all contracts for goods and services between consumers and traders, regardless of whether the contract is entered into in-store, distance or off-premises. The provisions of the Directive relating to distance and off-premises contracts are proposed in a new law on disclosure and withdrawal. The proposal for a completely new law is due to the need for extensive structural changes in the existing law. The legislative proposal does not reduce the high level of consumer protection resulting from existing legislation. The purpose of the new law is, like the law in force, to ensure that consumers receive relevant and necessary pre-contractual information in the case of distance and off-premises sales. Examples of sales outside regular business premises are street sales, trade fair sales and out-of-office door sales. Remote sales are defined as sales using remote communications, e.g. TV, Internet and telephone. The bill contains requirements for the contract and, as applicable, gives the consumer the right to withdraw from the contract within 14 days. The exceptions from the scope of the current law are proposed further. In accordance with that Directive, it is also proposed to exempt agreements covered by the Package Travel and Travel Guarantee Act – Package Travel Act – agreements on passenger services and certain agreements concluded with eco-providers. Agreements in these areas are well covered by other legislation. After amendments to the law, consumers are required to provide further information before the conclusion of a contract. The rules are simplified by common rules on the right of withdrawal for contracts concluded at distance and off-premises sales. There will also be common rules for goods and services. There will be stricter requirements for the trader, such as: may not charge the costs not declared before the conclusion of the contract. It is also proposed to increase the right to cancel the purchase of goods, even if it cannot be returned in the same condition and quantity. The proposal also proposes that pre-contractual information be provided to consumers in areas which are not covered by the proposal for a new law on disclosure and right of withdrawal. Another proposal is to limit the trader’s access to charges and additional payment for agreed performance, as well as provisions on the time of delivery when this is not agreed, and the transition from risk to purchase. The main changes in new law compared to the current law are a common legislation on goods and services, extended access to cancel purchases of goods, although they have been reduced, extended the obligation for the consumer to pay for the return of goods. As a general rule, the consumer shall return the benefit received before the consideration paid to the trader is received in return. Violation of certain information requirements has financial consequences for the trader. The information and right of withdrawal rules also apply when the consumer summons the trader, unless the total remuneration does not exceed NOK 1500. Remote sales by telephone are treated in the same way as other distance sales. Provisions on enforcement and penalties are included in the law. There shall be increased requirements for information from the trader prior to the conclusion of the contract. The specific provisions implementing the Remote Selling Directive on financial services are brought together in one part of the Act. Today, when the Family and Culture Committee presents a unanimous opinion, this is an expression of the fact that consumer rights are well anchored in Norwegian society. The Committee is convinced that the new law will guarantee good consumer rights in the future.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e4029	The Right of Withdrawal Act is now being harmonised with other EU and EEA countries, and it will both strengthen consumer rights and facilitate cross-border trade through more uniform rules. Consumer protection is important. Although Norway has strong rights for both consumers and traders, it is still of great importance that we ensure that we have instruments and legislation in place over time. Both the consumption pattern and the operators are in constant change and development. Therefore, we must have laws that simplify and are adapted to different forms of purchase and sale between the consumer and the trader. This is a law that should be easily accessible and easy to understand, as it regulates an area of great public interest. The purpose of the law is to ensure that the consumer is provided with relevant and necessary pre-contractual information in case of distance or off-premises sales. This area is so important for consumers, and this law will have such an interest, that there is reason to stress the Committee's comment on the need for a pedagogical guide, in order to make the main changes of the law easily available. In the consumer-business meeting, we talk about the consumer most and most easily. There is equal reason to recall that there are two parties to be taken into account, both the consumer and the trader. In this case, there will be some stricter requirements for operators. However, the simplification and harmonisation of cross-border trade will also benefit traders. We also see that some exceptions continue. Areas where derogations are proposed to be continued, including: for package travel and passenger transport services, as we heard, are well covered by other legislation. At the same time, we get a simplification by making provisions on the right of withdrawal common to contracts concluded in the case of distance sales and in the case of sales outside premises. With a constantly changing consumption pattern, it is a point to have as simplified and dynamic legislation as possible to ensure consumer rights.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e4042	The Government has submitted a proposal for a new law on disclosure and the right of withdrawal and proposals for amendments to the Marketing Act and the Contracts Act, the Financial Contracts Act, the Consumer Purchases Act and the Consumer Dispute Act. The proposal for a completely new law is due to the fact that the directives require extensive structural changes to the existing law. The legislative proposal will not reduce the high level of consumer protection resulting from the existing law. The purpose of a new law is, like the law in force, to ensure that consumers receive relevant and necessary pre-contractual information in the case of distance and off-premises sales. The legislative proposal implements the EU Consumer Rights Directive. The aim of the Directive is to contribute to the smooth functioning of the internal market by facilitating increased cross-border trade. It provides a high level of consumer protection. Since the Stortinget agreed to incorporate the Directive into the EEA Agreement, it was clear that Norwegian consumers would largely retain or obtain better rights, but that consumer protection might also be impaired in some respects. An e-commerce report from 2013 shows that nine out of ten have traded online, and that more than half of all Norwegians have traded a physical item from a foreign online store. It provides certainty for consumers and predictability for traders when the same rights and obligations apply, regardless of the European country in which they act. Norwegian consumers also purchase goods and services when, for example: are on a summer holiday, a long-term holiday or a study stay in other European countries. They will then benefit from the good rights conferred on them by the implementation of the Directive in all European countries. A significant point for Norwegian consumers is that the actual 14-day withdrawal period is continued. In the case of improvements, the new withdrawal law will also have a simpler structure than the existing law, with fewer exceptions. For example, common requirements are proposed for agreements on goods and services and one system for implementing the right of withdrawal. In addition, the consumer is required to have more pre-contractual information than today. Some information requirements are due to the digital development of society and consumers are required to know the trader’s e-mail address. Another example is information on digital content, for example: whether an e-book can be used on an iPad. A further improvement with a new right of withdrawal is that the trader cannot charge the costs covered where this has not been stated before the agreement was concluded. Therefore, the trader must himself cover additional costs and return costs where this is not stated before trading. To the assistance of the trader, an information form is introduced to ensure that the trader provides such information to the consumer. Most of the provisions of the Directive are fully harmonised, i.e. that no national rules derogating from them have been granted access. However, some provisions are optional, and I am pleased to propose that the specific protection of consumers in the event of unsolicited telephone sales be continued. This means that traders calling a consumer to sell something must confirm their offer in writing after the telephone call and that the consumer is not bound until this offer has been accepted in writing. As mentioned above, the Directive also leads to a slight weakening of protection for Norwegian consumers in a few respects. The consumer will now have to pay for the return of goods when using the right of withdrawal in all cases, if the trader has informed about this cost. Today it is the trader who has to pay for the return on telephone sales and contracts that have not been concluded through the store. In addition, after a new right of withdrawal, the consumer has to pay more for work carried out until the consumer exercises the right of withdrawal. An example of this is if the consumer asks the trader to start painting a house immediately after the conclusion of a contract and the consumer subsequently repents. The new rules also provide some practical simplifications. There will now be one withdrawal form common to non-financial goods and services. I note that the Committee believes that a pedagogical guide to the law on the right of withdrawal should be established. After reading the whole matter, I see that it is a necessity. It's a very technical and complicated law. But I can also tell Stortinget that the Consumer Council has been commissioned by the department to create such a supervisor. It will be posted online when the law enters into force and will benefit both consumers and traders. Overall, the implementation of the Consumer Rights Directive strengthens consumer rights.

Section 5 of the Neighborhood Act states that no one must start digging, building, blasting, etc. without securing neighboring property. Section 6 goes further. Under the heading ‘Guidance Notice and Pre-Decisions’ it is stated that when someone wants to start planting, digging, construction, industrial activity or other activities, he must notify his neighbour in a reasonable time in advance. This requires the municipality to have good control plans, usually with specified building heights and building lines and other municipal technical conditions, such as: securing against construction over water and sewerage. With regard to regulatory plans, all landowners, charities and stakeholders are entitled to an opinion. We respond to criticism that has been raised over several years, from industry, from municipalities and from residents. The goal is a simpler and more predictable planning and building law. The goal is more and more affordable housing and less bureaucracy. The Planning and Building Act shall provide for predictability and ensure quality. Since the Planning and Building Act as a whole is being brought together in the Ministry of Municipal Affairs and Modernisation, we have been given a greater opportunity to see the context of the entire planning and building process and to provide a better basis for the simplification measures that are now being implemented. We want to contribute to more efficient planning and construction processes, fewer rematches and more predictability for both industry, municipality and residents. We're doing a great job of simplification. We facilitate fewer objections by placing greater emphasis on local democracy, by shorter deadlines and by raising the threshold of objections. We have now provided for simplifications in the Building Act, for the plan part and also in the Building Technical Regulation. The proposals we are considering today contribute to new and shorter deadlines for the municipal proceedings. We exempt more measures from application obligations, and we contribute to fewer redundancies and less bureaucracy. Several of the new deadlines are important. Among other things, we propose that simple construction projects for which people themselves can be responsible, for example. housing additions up to 50 m2 shall be allowed a period of up to three weeks. Today, there is an automatic way that neighbouring protests lead to an extension of up to 12 weeks. We believe that authorisation should be regarded as granted if the municipality has not dealt with the matter within three weeks. We also propose to exempt further measures from the obligation to apply. It is perhaps the case that has created the most discussion. We believe that there is a reasonable simplification measure which obviously requires the action holder to deal with the laws and regulations and the municipal regulatory plans. We also want to consult a new regulation that will also require both height, length and other issues that are important in a neighbouring relationship. These matters currently constitute 20 pst. of the construction materials of the municipalities. This means that when these cases are taken out of the formal obligation to apply, municipalities will be freed of capacity so that they can invest more in the large and complex issues and can also be a better guide for the citizens. I record that the red-green parties, with the exception of SV, stick to their original proposal. It is a proposal that was more radical or more extreme than the government has set up, without seeking to start building up to 50 m2, 70 m2 of garage or 500 m2 of farmland. Although a formal neighbouring alert was maintained, municipalities or action holders were not obliged to take account of this neighbouring alert. I note that the representative, Mr. Greni, says that the requirement for a neighbouring alert will reduce the risk of neighbouring conflicts. I can quote a head of a building committee who said: Today there is a complaint about almost everything – it cannot be worse than it is today! The system we have does not help to reduce neighboring conflicts. We believe that the proposal we have put forward will contribute both to more predictability and to other ways of resolving the conflicts. Let me conclude by saying that with the proposal we are promoting, in the light of the EEA requirement for local approval, we are committed to the same thing as the other parties in Stortinget, namely ensuring seriousness and quality. We work well with the construction industry to find an alternative to the local approval.

ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e4547	The presentation of the case in this case we have to deal with now, has in a good way provided a broad review of the law on compensation for natural damage, in line with St. prp. No 1 for 2004–2005. There has been no full review of the Natural Damage Act since the law was first adopted in 1961, and it has been high time to put this matter on the agenda and make any necessary changes. The proposals involve significant simplification and strengthening, for the benefit of all parties. The Committee, through its examination, has expressed clear support for the main line of the proposal, as well as a clear majority of the hearing bodies. There are several amendments in the proposal, but I would like to draw up a few of them, which the Committee has also expressed in particular. In the proposal, a shift away from the system of wage-earners for the establishment of tariffs and to a purely administrative model with the State's agricultural management as a national administrative body. This helps both to free up police resources and to ensure more effective treatment, but perhaps what is at least equally important: a faster payment of compensation. Today, a currant case may have a treatment period of four to six months, while the new model is estimated to contribute to a halving, and we have to say that we are reasonably satisfied with that. At the same time, it is gratifying that landowners and other rightholders are also included in this scheme. The Committee shares the view of the government on the importance of opening up further regulation of recourse requirements and advance warning of recourse requirements. A recourse requirement may in some cases be applicable to municipalities. Regression to municipalities, for example, may be relevant where building permits have been granted in a racial or flooded area. Therefore, further regulation of the possibility of recourse is necessary in the view of the Committee, including ensuring that the third party is entitled to express an opinion prior to a possible recourse. Finally, I am pleased that there is a great deal of consensus in the Committee on the amendments proposed in the law on compensation for natural damage.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e4564	New law on compensation for damage mainly involves procedural changes compared to current legislation. The material content of the scheme has largely continued, of course, with some changes and codification of established practices. This is a legal work with which the previous government worked a lot, and is allowed to become the first agricultural matter from this government that is being treated in Stortinget. There is every reason to support the law and proposals contained therein — and this is reflected in the unanimous position of the Committee. However, it is not that a new law will make conflicts disappear or that some will not feel unfairly treated when natural damage occurs. There may be questions of compensation measurement, of the separation between recovery and protection, of compensation for rescue costs and of the recovery of additional costs in order to reduce the risk of future damage to the natural environment. The new law does not mean that all difficult assessments disappear, but hopefully it will be much quicker to clarify the issues and get the money on book. Then I'm told that we're not just going to paint the situation that's been using the lumbar sex. It is important to stress the importance of local knowledge in this type of issue and that it is also necessary to strive to include local knowledge in the process with a new model. Several of the difficult questions will be clarified in the more detailed regulatory work, and I have taken the floor today to highlight some of the issues that I believe are central. Firstly, the proportion of those who claim compensation for several properties is central. Here it is the view of the Centre Party that a cap must be placed on a protrusion when the regulation is to be laid down. The proposition says that the new scheme should not substantially change the costs, but I would also like to highlight what it says is to be seen as ‘reasonable and fair’. We expect the government to follow up on this, because it is difficult to say that five excesses of one damage are fair and fair. Then the centre party is concerned that, in establishing regulations on rescue costs, considerable understanding is to be given to injured parties and that the regulations do not become too system-friendly. The centre party and the Workers' Party have also stated in the comments that the right to recourse must be designed and practised in a way that does not give undue expression to the municipalities, and that we are concerned that this will be preserved in the further process.
ParlaMint-NO_2014-06-16.ana.ud1585e4583	I am happy to be here today with a bill that has a social importance. I am also pleased that the whole Stortinge agrees with this. The main objective of revising the Natural Damage Act is to create a simpler, more efficient and forward-looking law. Furthermore, the legislative proposal provides for a better legal structure and a clearer distinction between the existing rules on protection against natural damage and the rules on compensation for natural damage. The State compensation scheme for natural damage is currently governed by the Law on Natural Damage of l994. The law has undergone significant changes over the years without a full review since the first law was passed in 1961. Applications for natural damages are dealt with in two stages. As a general rule, the damage is first assessed by a member of the community, then a decision on compensation is taken by the board of the State Natural Damage Fund. There are complaints/anomalies in each track. Experience from major natural disasters involving many victims shows that the current system is inefficient and leads to a long process. Figures for the period 2004–2013 show that, on average, approximately 1 350 natural damage cases are dealt with annually, while annual payments are on average higher than 100 million. kr. The number of annual claims and the amount of compensation payments may vary widely from year to year, depending on the size of natural accidents in the previous year. In recent years, the extent of damage also suggests that the State should have a more robust and forward-looking compensation system capable of addressing the challenges in a more flexible and faster way than the current system provides. The legislative proposal goes on to a large extent the material content of today's law on natural damage, but the proposal involves a great degree of simplification, by replacing today's two-track model on natural damage with a purely management model. This leads to shorter procedures so that the victim is paid more quickly than under the present system. According to the proposal, the workers will no longer have the task of appreciating natural damage. Harm-reduction is a task that requires large resources in the districts of lenners that are exposed to natural damage. It is not appropriate for the police to use their resources for such work. This is also evident from the conclusion of the police analysis that has been carried out. This proposal will thus release resources in the police that can be used for regular police work. The proposal will facilitate the deployment of additional resources in the event of major natural disasters. The examination of all cases in one responsible body will ensure uniform treatment and contribute to increased legal certainty for each victim. The legislative proposal further states that the circle of the beneficiaries is extended from the owner only to the landowners and other rightholders who can prove to have the economic interest in, and the availability of, the damaged object. This applies regardless of whether the damage is on private or public land. Finally, I would like to mention that, overall, the bill will mean administrative savings of about 11 million. kr annually compared to today’s scheme.
